I feel that awful tightness in my chest. It's a very real, very physical thing. When I have to put my razor down on the edge of the sink, I can feel the cold porcelain under my fingers. Or if I'm at the kitchen table, I can feel the hard wood of the chair against my back. \n\nI don't lose track of the world around me, either. I'm fully aware of it. I can still hear the refrigerator humming in the background, or the clock ticking in the hallway—we have this old grandfather clock my father left me, it's got a very heavy, loud tick to it, you have to wind it every eight days—and I hear that clear as a bell while I'm sitting there trying to catch my breath. So, no, I wouldn't use the word detached. I know exactly where I am and who I am. I'm just an old fellow trying to get through his morning shave or his painting, waiting for his ribs to stop feeling like they're tied up in knots so he can get on with his day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're actually back in the workshop, reliving the argument, with all the sights and sounds coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Doc, with all due respect, I... I feel like we're going around in circles here. I really do. I know you've got your list of questions there, and you have to ask them, but I feel like I've answered this one two or three times already today. \n\nLike I was just saying—and I'll try to say it as plain as I can—no, I don't feel like I'm back in the workshop. I don't hear the yelling. I don't hear the tools clanking or the echo in that old tin-roof shed. It's not a movie. It's just that one picture of his hand holding the tool. \n\nI'm right here in my house, or wherever I happen to be, just trying to go about my business. I'll be right in the middle of shaving, like I mentioned, with that badger hair brush and the shaving soap—it's a sandalwood soap, comes in a wooden puck, very nice scent, my daughter gets it for me every Christmas—and I'm standing there, fully aware that I'm in my own bathroom. Or I'm at the kitchen table with my watercolors, trying to get a wash just right on some heavy-weight cold-pressed paper. I know exactly where I am. \n\nBut then that image flashes, and my chest gets tight, and I just have to sit there and breathe through it. I hear the grandfather clock ticking, I hear the refrigerator hum. I am not back in the workshop. I'm just an old man sitting in his kitchen with a tight chest, waiting for it to pass so I can pick my paintbrush back up. I really don't know how else to make it any clearer for you, Doc. It's not the whole scene, it's just the one picture.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or daily routines that you avoid because they remind you of the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, there are. And frankly, Doc, that's exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because it's getting to be too much, trying to tiptoe around my own life. \n\nIt's the workshop. My own workshop. I used to spend hours out there. I've got a beautiful old Delta table saw, a drill press, a whole wall of hand tools I've collected over fifty years... and I just can't bring myself to go out there anymore. I'll look out the back window at it, and I just... I steer clear. I'll find any excuse not to go in. \"Oh, the grass needs cutting,\" or \"I should really clean the gutters today,\" anything to avoid opening that shed door. Because I know if I go in there, I'm just going to be thinking about that afternoon, standing right where it happened.\n\nAnd it's not just the shop, either. It's the family get-togethers. My sister—bless her heart, she tries to keep the family close—she hosts a barbecue every Fourth of July, Thanksgiving dinner, Christmas Eve. She goes all out. Bakes these incredible sweet potato pies from our mother's old recipe. But her husband is my brother-in-law, obviously. So if there's even a chance he's going to be there, I just... I make an excuse. I tell them my arthritis is acting up, or I've got a summer cold, or I just don't feel up to the drive. \n\nAnd it breaks my heart, Doc, it really does. I miss seeing my nieces and nephews, I miss my sister's cooking. But the thought of sitting across the table from him, or even just being in the same room and seeing his hands passing the gravy boat... I can't do it. I've been planning my whole life around not running into him, and not going into my own workshop, and after all this time, I'm just plain tired of it. It's exhausting, keeping track of where I can and can't go just to avoid those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you avoid because they remind you of what happened with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, honestly... no. Not really any other places. I... I feel like I just laid it all out for you, to be perfectly frank. Like I was just saying, the whole reason I finally picked up the phone and made this appointment to come in and see you in the first place is because of those two things I just went on and on about. \n\nIt's the workshop, and it's any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be. And I know that might not sound like a long list of places to you—maybe you have folks in here who can't even leave their bedrooms or go to the grocery store—but for me, at seventy-six years old, that's a huge chunk of my world. \n\nI mean, you have to understand the toll it takes, constantly being on guard like that. I am just so terribly tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. It's like a full-time job, keeping track of it all. I'll look at the calendar on the fridge—the one the bank gives out for free every January—and I'll see a holiday coming up, or a niece's birthday, and instead of getting excited to see my sister, my stomach just drops. Because I have to start calculating if he's going to be there, and what excuse I'm going to use this time so I don't have to go. 'Oh, my sciatica is flaring up,' or 'the Buick is making a funny noise.' \n\nAnd the shop... well, I already told you about the shop. I just keep steering clear of it entirely, letting the dust settle on all my good tools. So, no, there aren't really any *other* places. Just my own backyard and my own family, which, if you ask me, is more than enough to lose. I just want to stop having to map out my days trying to dodge him and that shed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Doc, I... I really don't mean to be difficult here, but I swear we just went over this exact same thing. I know you've got your clipboard and your checklist, but I feel like I'm repeating myself, and frankly, it's getting a little frustrating. 

Like I said just a few minutes ago—and I'll say it again, as plain as day—no, I don't feel like I'm reliving it. I don't feel like it's happening right now. I'm sitting right here in this chair in your office. I can see the degrees on your wall, I can hear the traffic out on the street. I know it's Tuesday morning. 

When that image pops into my head, that picture of him gripping that wrench so tight his knuckles were white... it's just a picture. A memory. A very clear, very upsetting memory that makes my chest tighten up, sure, but I never once think I'm actually back in that tin-roof shed. I don't lose touch with reality, Doc. I'm seventy-six years old, my knees ache when it rains, and I know exactly where I am. I'm not traveling back in time. It's just a flash in my mind's eye, and then I just have to sit there, whether I'm shaving or painting or whatever, and wait for my heart to stop racing. I really, truly don't know how to explain it any better than that. It's not happening again. It's just a memory that won't leave me be.

---------- turn 14 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I suppose I’m here because I just... I can’t keep doing this dance anymore. You see, I’ve got this workshop out back. Built it myself, oh, must have been twenty years ago now, right around the time I stopped working. I used to spend just about every afternoon out there, tinkering with things, staying busy. It was my favorite place to be. But for a good while now, I just... I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop. I won't even walk past the door if I can help it, I'll take the long way around the yard just to avoid looking at it. \n\nAnd it’s not just the shop, it’s my own family, too. If there's a family get-together—a Sunday dinner, a birthday, whatever it might be—I find myself interrogating everybody beforehand to see if my brother-in-law is going to be there. If there is even a slight chance he might show up, I make up some excuse about my back acting up and I just don't go. I stay home. And I’m 76 years old... after all this time, I am just so flat-out tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. It’s exhausting, having to map out every single day, every weekend, just to avoid a room in my own backyard or a dinner with my own relatives. It's just no way to live, always having to plan my schedule around what I can't be near.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you think about going into your workshop or seeing your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s a funny thing, Doc, and not funny haha, you know? It’s just... when I even so much as let my mind drift toward that shed, or toward him, it’s like a switch flips. But the worst part is, I don't even have to be actively thinking about going out there or seeing him for it to hit me. I could be doing just about anything, the most mundane things you can imagine. \n\nTake the mornings, for instance. I’ll be standing there at the bathroom sink, right? Just shaving. I use a brush and a soap puck, the old-fashioned way, always have, ever since I was a teenager. The water is running, nice and warm, and I'm just looking in the mirror, minding my own business, and then... out of nowhere, I'll see his hand. I see his hand gripping that heavy tool again. It’s exactly like a picture that just pops right into my head, clear as day. \n\nOr, you know, I've been trying to do some painting lately. Just small watercolors at the kitchen table to keep my hands busy since I can't go out to the workshop anymore. I'll be sitting there, mixing a color, maybe trying to get a nice sky blue, and suddenly that same picture flashes right in front of my eyes. Just his hand, and that tool. And when it does... oh, boy. My chest just tightens right up. It gets so tight, like someone wrapped a belt around my ribs and pulled it taut, and I usually have to put the razor down, or the paintbrush down, and just grip the edge of the counter or the table until it passes. It’s just... it's always that same image, and it just won't leave me be.\n\nDoctor: What happens to your mood when you see that picture of your brother-in-law's hand in your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, mood... now that’s a tricky word, Doc. I mean, if you’re asking if I get down in the dumps or start crying, or if I get mad enough to kick the dog—not that I have a dog anymore, our old golden retriever Buster passed away about five years ago, bless his heart. Best dog a man could ask for, he used to sit right at my feet while I drank my morning coffee and read the paper. \n\nBut anyway, getting back to my mood... I can’t rightly say it changes my mood, per se. I don't suddenly feel sad or angry or anything like that. It’s not like when the grandkids come over and leave a mess in the living room and I get a little grumpy for the rest of the afternoon, you know? I just... I don't really notice a specific emotion like that, outside of that terrible tightness in my ribs I was just telling you about. \n\nIt’s just that picture flashing, and then that grip in my chest, and I just have to stand there and wait for it to pass. So as far as my actual mood goes—like being happy or sad or irritable or what have you—I honestly couldn't tell you. I just haven't really noticed anything like that happening. It’s more just... it happens, and then once my chest loosens up, I just go right back to trying to finish my shave or my watercolor. I don't think my mood really factors into it at all, to be perfectly honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of your brother-in-law or something he did that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I suppose it's... it's a bit of both, but mostly it's what happened, or at least what I can remember of what happened. You see, about three years ago, we had this big family barbecue. It was the Fourth of July, hotter than blazes out there, and everybody was over. My sister, her husband—that's the brother-in-law I'm talking about—the kids, the neighbors. We were all out back near the workshop. \n\nNow, he and I, we've never seen eye to eye. We were arguing about something foolish, probably politics or how to properly grill a steak, you know how those things start. But it got heated. Real heated. We ended up inside the workshop, just the two of us, away from the women and the kids. And the shouting... oh, I remember the shouting. It echoed off the tin roof in there. He was red in the face, veins popping out on his neck, screaming at me. \n\nAnd I remember him picking up that heavy wrench. That's the hand I keep seeing, gripping that wrench so tight his knuckles were white. I remember that plain as day. But the funny thing is... well, not funny, but the strange thing is, after that... nothing. It's just a blank spot. I remember the shouting and the tool plain enough, but there's a blank spot right around how it actually ended, like somebody cut a few minutes out of the story. \n\nOne minute he's holding the wrench and yelling, and the next thing I know, I'm sitting on a lawn chair outside, holding a bag of frozen peas to the side of my head, and my sister is crying. I don't remember him swinging it, I don't remember getting hit, I don't remember how I got out of the shop. The doctor at the ER said I had a mild concussion, said it's normal to lose a few minutes of memory with a knock to the head like that. But it's that missing piece, Doc. It's that blank spot. That's what I'm trying to avoid. Because if I go in that shop, or if I see him, I'm terrified that missing piece is going to come flooding back, and I just don't think my old heart could take remembering whatever it is I've forgotten about that day. So I just stay away. It's easier that way, don't you think? Just keeping the whole mess tucked away where I don't have to look at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about what happened with your brother-in-law on that Fourth of July?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as talking or thinking about it goes, I suppose I do try to push it right out of my mind whenever it creeps up, but... well, the real issue, the whole reason I finally made the appointment to come in and sit in this chair and talk to you today, is because of what I'm doing to avoid it all in my day-to-day life. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop. And I mean, I built that shed with my own two hands back in '98, Doc. I poured the concrete foundation myself, framed the walls, even ran the electrical so I could have my lathe and my band saw out there. It used to be my sanctuary, you know? And now... now I'll walk all the way around the long side of the yard, squeezing past where my wife used to keep her prize rose bushes, just so I don't even have to look at the door of the place. \n\nAnd it’s not just the shop, either. It’s the family stuff. Any get-together, any holiday dinner, any birthday party for the nieces and nephews... if there is even a whisper, even a slight chance that my brother-in-law might be there, I just don't go. I make up an excuse. I’ll tell my sister my arthritis is acting up, or I’ve got a plumbing leak to fix, or I just don't feel up to the drive. And I hate lying to her, I really do. She makes this wonderful potato salad, the kind with the red potatoes and the fresh dill, and I haven't had it in three years. \n\nBut after all this time... I'm just tired, Doc. I am so dog-tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. I'm exhausted from mapping out my own backyard and dodging my own family just to make sure I don't see him or that shop. It's like a full-time job, and at my age, I just don't have the energy for it anymore. That's why I came in. I just can't keep living my life tiptoeing around all these landmines.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places or situations, besides your workshop and family gatherings, that you avoid because they remind you of what happened with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that for a second... no, no, not really. Like I was just telling you, it’s mainly just the workshop and the family gatherings. But honestly, Doc, you have to understand that at my age, those two things take up a mighty big chunk of a man's life. When you're retired, you don't have a whole lot of places you have to be every day. Your world gets a little smaller, a little closer to home. So when I say I'm steering clear of my own workshop out back, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might show his face, well, that's pretty much my whole social calendar right there. \n\nI know I'm repeating myself, but that's exactly why I finally came in to see you today. It's just those two things, but they're everywhere. You wouldn't believe the mental gymnastics it takes to avoid your own backyard. Just the other day, for instance, the hinge on the cabinet under the kitchen sink came loose. The door was hanging all crooked. Now, all my good tools are out in that shop. Every last one of them, organized on a pegboard I put up myself. But instead of just walking thirty feet out the back door to grab a simple Phillips head screwdriver, I actually got my keys, got in my car, drove fifteen minutes down to the hardware store on Main Street, and bought a brand new one. I paid six dollars and forty-nine cents for a tool I already own three of, just so I wouldn't have to go near that shed door. \n\nNow that's just pure foolishness, isn't it? But that's what I'm doing. So, no, there aren't really other places or situations. It's just like I said—I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop and any family get-together where he might be. And after all this time, I am just so tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. It wears a man down, it really does.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the argument with your brother-in-law, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's like a movie playing the whole argument from start to finish, where I'm hearing all the shouting and the echoes in the tin roof again, if that's what you mean. But... well, it's more like a photograph. Out of nowhere, I'll just see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just his hand, gripping it so tight. \n\nAnd it happens at the absolute strangest times, Doc. Times when I'm not even thinking about him or the Fourth of July or any of that mess. Like... well, I could just be standing at the bathroom sink, shaving. I still use one of those old double-edge safety razors, the kind my own father taught me to use, so you really have to pay attention to what you're doing so you don't nick yourself. I'll be looking in the mirror, scraping through the shaving cream, trying to get a close shave around the jawline, and suddenly, like a picture that pops right into my head, there's that hand and that wrench. \n\nOr I'll be working on a painting. I took up watercoloring a while back, mostly sitting at the kitchen table since I don't go out to the shop anymore, just to keep my mind and hands occupied in my retirement. I'll be trying to mix the right shade of green for a tree or something, completely at peace, minding my own business, and then—bam—that image just forces its way in. \n\nAnd I'll tell you, even though it's just a flash in my mind, it still makes my chest tighten up something awful. It gets so tight, like a band is wrapping right around my ribs, that I actually have to put my razor or my paintbrush down and just sit there for a minute. I have to take these long, deep breaths until my heart settles down and the feeling passes. So, I don't know if that counts as reliving the whole argument exactly, but it sure feels like that one terrifying piece of it is happening right there in my kitchen or my bathroom, completely uninvited.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the workshop, reliving the argument with your brother-in-law, with all the sights and sounds coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, Doc, it's not really like I'm standing right back on the sawdust floor hearing all the yelling and the racket. The sounds don't really come back to me like a movie playing out, if that makes sense. It's not the whole scene. I guess I'll try to explain it again, because it's really just that one specific picture. \n\nOut of absolutely nowhere, I'll just see his hand gripping that heavy tool again. Just the hand and the tool, nothing else. And like I mentioned, it sneaks up on me when my mind is completely elsewhere, doing the most mundane things. I could be standing there at the bathroom mirror in the morning, getting my shaving cream all lathered up in my old ceramic mug—the one with the badger hair brush my wife bought me for our anniversary twenty years ago—just trying to get a clean shave around my chin. Or I'll be sitting at the kitchen table, working on one of my watercolors. I might be right in the middle of trying to get the shading right on a landscape, holding a tiny little detail brush, completely relaxed, not a care in the world... \n\nAnd then, boom, that picture just pops right into my head. Just his hand with that heavy tool. And even though there's no sound, no yelling, no echo in the shed... it still does a number on me physically. It still makes my chest tighten up so bad. It gets so tight, like a heavy strap is being pulled around my ribs, that I just have to set my paintbrush down, or put the razor on the edge of the sink, and just sit there for a spell. I have to just breathe through it until my chest stops feeling like it's in a vice grip. So, no, I don't feel like I'm completely back in the workshop with all the noise, but that one image... well, it's enough to knock the wind right out of me, I'll tell you that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're actually back in the moment when your brother-in-law was holding the wrench, like you're reliving it?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... well, I feel like a bit of a broken record here, to be honest with you. Like I've been trying to say, it's not like I'm fully transported back there in body and spirit, reliving the whole afternoon. It's exactly what I was just telling you about. It's just that one picture. \n\nOut of absolutely nowhere, I'll see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just the hand, gripping it. And it pops right into my head like a photograph, usually when I'm doing something completely peaceful and unrelated. Like I said, it happens while I'm shaving with my old safety razor, or when I'm sitting at the kitchen table working on a painting. I could be mixing a nice cerulean blue for a sky, minding my own business, and then—bam. There it is. \n\nAnd I know I already mentioned this, but it's the part that really gets to me, so it bears repeating... it still makes my chest tighten up. It gets so terribly tight I have to put down whatever I'm holding, whether it's the razor or the paintbrush, and just sit and take deep breaths until it passes. So, I don't know if you'd call that actually being back in the moment, but it sure forces its way into my day, plain as day. I really don't know how else to explain it to you, Doc, it's just that one flash, over and over, and it knocks the wind right out of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you when that picture of your brother-in-law's hand pops into your head?\n\nPatient: Detached? From myself? Well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I think I know what you mean. You hear about folks who go through something and they talk about feeling like they're floating outside their body, or looking down at themselves from the ceiling like a ghost or something you'd see on one of those old Twilight Zone programs. But for me, it's not like that at all. \n\nIf anything, Doc, I feel a little too stuck in my own body, if you catch my drift. Because like I was just saying—and I know I keep harping on it, but it's the truth—when that picture of his hand pops into my head, I am very much right there in my own skin. I feel that awful tightness in my chest. It's a very real, very physical thing. When I have to put my razor down on the edge of the sink, I can feel the cold porcelain under my fingers. Or if I'm at the kitchen table, I can feel the hard wood of the chair against my back. \n\nI don't lose track of the world around me, either. I'm fully aware of it. I can still hear the refrigerator humming in the background, or the clock ticking in the hallway—we have this old grandfather clock my father left me, it's got a very heavy, loud tick to it, you have to wind it every eight days—and I hear that clear as a bell while I'm sitting there trying to catch my breath. So, no, I wouldn't use the word detached. I know exactly where I am and who I am. I'm just an old fellow trying to get through his morning shave or his painting, waiting for his ribs to stop feeling like they're tied up in knots so he can get on with his day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're actually back in the workshop, reliving the argument, with all the sights and sounds coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Doc, with all due respect, I... I don't hear the yelling. I don't hear the tools clanking or the echo in that old tin-roof shed. It's not a movie. It's just that one picture of his hand holding the tool. \n\nI'm right here in my house, or wherever I happen to be, just trying to go about my business. I'll be right in the middle of shaving, like I mentioned, with that badger hair brush and the shaving soap—it's a sandalwood soap, comes in a wooden puck, very nice scent, my daughter gets it for me every Christmas—and I'm standing there, fully aware that I'm in my own bathroom. I know exactly where I am. \n\nBut then that image flashes, and my chest gets tight, and I just have to sit there and breathe through it. I hear the grandfather clock ticking, I hear the refrigerator hum. I am not back in the workshop. I'm just an old man sitting in his kitchen with a tight chest, waiting for it to pass so I can pick my paintbrush back up. I really don't know how else to make it any clearer for you, Doc. It's not the whole scene, it's just the one picture.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or daily routines that you avoid because they remind you of the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, there are. And frankly, Doc, that's exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because it's getting to be too much, trying to tiptoe around my own life. \n\nIt's the workshop. My own workshop. I used to spend hours out there. I've got a beautiful old Delta table saw, a drill press, a whole wall of hand tools I've collected over fifty years... and I just can't bring myself to go out there anymore. I'll look out the back window at it, and I just... I steer clear. I'll find any excuse not to go in. \"Oh, the grass needs cutting,\" or \"I should really clean the gutters today,\" anything to avoid opening that shed door. My sister—bless her heart, she tries to keep the family close—she hosts a barbecue every Fourth of July, Thanksgiving dinner, Christmas Eve. She goes all out. Bakes these incredible sweet potato pies from our mother's old recipe. But her husband is my brother-in-law, obviously. So if there's even a chance he's going to be there, I just... I make an excuse. I tell them my arthritis is acting up, or I've got a summer cold, or I just don't feel up to the drive. \n\nAnd it breaks my heart, Doc, it really does. I miss seeing my nieces and nephews, I miss my sister's cooking. I've been planning my whole life around not running into him, and not going into my own workshop, and after all this time, I'm just plain tired of it. It's exhausting, keeping track of where I can and can't go just to avoid those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you avoid because they remind you of what happened with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, honestly... no. I'll look at the calendar on the fridge—the one the bank gives out for free every January—and I'll see a holiday coming up, or a niece's birthday, and instead of getting excited to see my sister, my stomach just drops. It gets so tight, like a band is wrapping right around my ribs, that I actually have to put my razor or my paintbrush down and just sit there for a minute. I have to take these long, deep breaths until my heart settles down and the feeling passes. So, I don't know if that counts as reliving the whole argument exactly, but it sure feels like that one terrifying piece of it is happening right there in my kitchen or my bathroom, completely uninvited.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the workshop, reliving the argument with your brother-in-law, with all the sights and sounds coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, Doc, it's not really like I'm standing right back on the sawdust floor hearing all the yelling and the racket. The sounds don't really come back to me like a movie playing out, if that makes sense. It's not the whole scene. I guess I'll try to explain it again, because it's really just that one specific picture. \n\nOut of absolutely nowhere, I'll just see his hand gripping that heavy tool again. Just the hand and the tool, nothing else. And like I mentioned, it sneaks up on me when my mind is completely elsewhere, doing the most mundane things. I could be standing there at the bathroom mirror in the morning, getting my shaving cream all lathered up in my old ceramic mug—the one with the badger hair brush my wife bought me for our anniversary twenty years ago—just trying to get a clean shave around my chin. Or I'll be sitting at the kitchen table, working on one of my watercolors. I might be right in the middle of trying to get the shading right on a landscape, holding a tiny little detail brush, completely relaxed, not a care in the world... \n\nAnd then, boom, that picture just pops right into my head. Just his hand with that heavy tool. And even though there's no sound, no yelling, no echo in the shed... it still does a number on me physically. It still makes my chest tighten up so bad. It gets so tight, like a heavy strap is being pulled around my ribs, that I just have to set my paintbrush down, or put the razor on the edge of the sink, and just sit there for a spell. I have to just breathe through it until my chest stops feeling like it's in a vice grip. So, no, I don't feel like I'm completely back in the workshop with all the noise, but that one image... well, it's enough to knock the wind right out of me, I'll tell you that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're actually back in the moment when your brother-in-law was holding the wrench, like you're reliving it?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... well, I feel like a bit of a broken record here, to be honest with you. Like I've been trying to say, it's not like I'm fully transported back there in body and spirit, reliving the whole afternoon. It's exactly what I was just telling you about. It's just that one picture. \n\nOut of absolutely nowhere, I'll see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just the hand, gripping it. And it pops right into my head like a photograph, usually when I'm doing something completely peaceful and unrelated. Like I said, it happens while I'm shaving with my old safety razor, or when I'm sitting at the kitchen table working on a painting. I could be mixing a nice cerulean blue for a sky, minding my own business, and then—bam. There it is. \n\nAnd I know I already mentioned this, but it's the part that really gets to me, so it bears repeating... it still makes my chest tighten up. It gets so terribly tight I have to put down whatever I'm holding, whether it's the razor or the paintbrush, and just sit and take deep breaths until it passes. So, I don't know if you'd call that actually being back in the moment, but it sure forces its way into my day, plain as day. I really don't know how else to explain it to you, Doc, it's just that one flash, over and over, and it knocks the wind right out of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you when that picture of your brother-in-law's hand pops into your head?\n\nPatient: Detached? From myself? Well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I think I know what you mean. You hear about folks who go through something and they talk about feeling like they're floating outside their body, or looking down at themselves from the ceiling like a ghost or something you'd see on one of those old Twilight Zone programs. But for me, it's not like that at all. \n\nIf anything, Doc, I feel a little too stuck in my own body, if you catch my drift. Because like I was just saying—and I know I keep harping on it, but it's the truth—when that picture of his hand pops into my head, I am very much right there in my own skin. I feel that awful tightness in my chest. It's a very real, very physical thing. When I have to put my razor down on the edge of the sink, I can feel the cold porcelain under my fingers. Or if I'm at the kitchen table, I can feel the hard wood of the chair against my back. \n\nI don't lose track of the world around me, either. I'm fully aware of it. I can still hear the refrigerator humming in the background, or the clock ticking in the hallway—we have this old grandfather clock my father left me, it's got a very heavy, loud tick to it, you have to wind it every eight days—and I hear that clear as a bell while I'm sitting there trying to catch my breath. So, no, I wouldn't use the word detached. I know exactly where I am and who I am. I'm just an old fellow trying to get through his morning shave or his painting, waiting for his ribs to stop feeling like they're tied up in knots so he can get on with his day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're actually back in the workshop, reliving the argument, with all the sights and sounds coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Doc, with all due respect, I... I feel like we're going around in circles here. I really do. I know you've got your list of questions there, and you have to ask them, but I feel like I've answered this one two or three times already today. \n\nLike I was just saying—and I'll try to say it as plain as I can—no, I don't feel like I'm back in the workshop. I don't hear the yelling. I don't hear the tools clanking or the echo in that old tin-roof shed. It's not a movie. It's just that one picture of his hand holding the tool. \n\nI'm right here in my house, or wherever I happen to be, just trying to go about my business. I'll be right in the middle of shaving, like I mentioned, with that badger hair brush and the shaving soap—it's a sandalwood soap, comes in a wooden puck, very nice scent, my daughter gets it for me every Christmas—and I'm standing there, fully aware that I'm in my own bathroom. Or I'm at the kitchen table with my watercolors, trying to get a wash just right on some heavy-weight cold-pressed paper. I know exactly where I am. \n\nBut then that image flashes, and my chest gets tight, and I just have to sit there and breathe through it. I hear the grandfather clock ticking, I hear the refrigerator hum. I am not back in the workshop. I'm just an old man sitting in his kitchen with a tight chest, waiting for it to pass so I can pick my paintbrush back up. I really don't know how else to make it any clearer for you, Doc. It's not the whole scene, it's just the one picture.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or daily routines that you avoid because they remind you of the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, there are. And frankly, Doc, that's exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because it's getting to be too much, trying to tiptoe around my own life. \n\nIt's the workshop. My own workshop. I used to spend hours out there. I've got a beautiful old Delta table saw, a drill press, a whole wall of hand tools I've collected over fifty years... and I just can't bring myself to go out there anymore. I'll look out the back window at it, and I just... I steer clear. I'll find any excuse not to go in. \"Oh, the grass needs cutting,\" or \"I should really clean the gutters today,\" anything to avoid opening that shed door. Because I know if I go in there, I'm just going to be thinking about that afternoon, standing right where it happened.\n\nAnd it's not just the shop, either. It's the family get-togethers. My sister—bless her heart, she tries to keep the family close—she hosts a barbecue every Fourth of July, Thanksgiving dinner, Christmas Eve. She goes all out. Bakes these incredible sweet potato pies from our mother's old recipe. But her husband is my brother-in-law, obviously. So if there's even a chance he's going to be there, I just... I make an excuse. I tell them my arthritis is acting up, or I've got a summer cold, or I just don't feel up to the drive. \n\nAnd it breaks my heart, Doc, it really does. I miss seeing my nieces and nephews, I miss my sister's cooking. But the thought of sitting across the table from him, or even just being in the same room and seeing his hands passing the gravy boat... I can't do it. I've been planning my whole life around not running into him, and not going into my own workshop, and after all this time, I'm just plain tired of it. It's exhausting, keeping track of where I can and can't go just to avoid those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you avoid because they remind you of what happened with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, honestly... no. Not really any other places. I... I feel like I just laid it all out for you, to be perfectly frank. Like I was just saying, the whole reason I finally picked up the phone and made this appointment to come in and see you in the first place is because of those two things I just went on and on about. \n\nIt's the workshop, and it's any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be. And I know that might not sound like a long list of places to you—maybe you have folks in here who can't even leave their bedrooms or go to the grocery store—but for me, at seventy-six years old, that's a huge chunk of my world. \n\nI mean, you have to understand the toll it takes, constantly being on guard like that. I am just so terribly tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. It's like a full-time job, keeping track of it all. I'll look at the calendar on the fridge—the one the bank gives out for free every January—and I'll see a holiday coming up, or a niece's birthday, and instead of getting excited to see my sister, my stomach just drops. Because I have to start calculating if he's going to be there, and what excuse I'm going to use this time so I don't have to go. 'Oh, my sciatica is flaring up,' or 'the Buick is making a funny noise.' \n\nAnd the shop... well, I already told you about the shop. I just keep steering clear of it entirely, letting the dust settle on all my good tools. So, no, there aren't really any *other* places. Just my own backyard and my own family, which, if you ask me, is more than enough to lose. I just want to stop having to map out my days trying to dodge him and that shed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you are actually reliving the moment when your brother-in-law was holding the wrench, as if it is happening again right now?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I really don't mean to be difficult here, but I swear we just went over this exact same thing. I know you've got your clipboard and your checklist, but I feel like I'm repeating myself, and frankly, it's getting a little frustrating. \n\nLike I said just a few minutes ago—and I'll say it again, as plain as day—no, I don't feel like I'm reliving it. I don't feel like it's happening right now. I'm sitting right here in this chair in your office. I can see the degrees on your wall, I can hear the traffic out on the street. I know it's Tuesday morning. \n\nWhen that image pops into my head, that picture of him gripping that wrench so tight his knuckles were white... it's just a picture. A memory. A very clear, very upsetting memory that makes my chest tighten up, sure, but I never once think I'm actually back in that tin-roof shed. I don't lose touch with reality, Doc. I'm seventy-six years old, my knees ache when it rains, and I know exactly where I am. I'm not traveling back in time. It's just a flash in my mind's eye, and then I just have to sit there, whether I'm shaving or painting or whatever, and wait for my heart to stop racing. I really, truly don't know how to explain it any better than that. It's not happening again. It's just a memory that won't leave me be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I... I really don't know how else to say this. I feel like we're just going around in circles here. I know you have to ask these things, but I swear I just answered this. I really did. 

Like I was saying, I don't feel like I'm back in the workshop. I'm not hearing the sounds of the saws or smelling the sawdust or any of that. I'm not reliving it like it's a movie playing around me. It's just... it's just this picture that pops into my head. Out of nowhere, really. I could be standing at the bathroom mirror, shaving, just trying to get the stubble off my chin, or I could be sitting in the living room working on one of my little watercolor paintings—I started doing watercolors a few years back, just landscapes mostly, barns and trees and such—and suddenly, bam. There it is. 

I'll just see his hand, plain as day. Just his hand gripped around that heavy tool. That big wrench. And it's just a flash, just an image in my mind, but it's enough to make my chest tighten up. My heart starts going a mile a minute, and I have to put down the razor or the paintbrush and just breathe for a second. But I know I'm in my bathroom. I know I'm in my living room. I'm not back in that shed, and I'm not hearing him yell. It's just that one picture of his hand that won't leave me alone. So, no, I'm not reliving it with all the sights and sounds. It's just that one awful image that keeps sneaking up on me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I... I really don't want to sound like a broken record here, but I swear you just asked me this exact same question a few minutes ago. I know you've got your list of things to get through, but I feel like I'm just repeating myself over and over. 

Like I was saying before, the whole reason I'm sitting here in this chair today, talking to you, is because of those two main things. I keep steering clear of my own workshop out back, and I absolutely refuse to go to any family get-together, any barbecue, any holiday dinner where my brother-in-law might be showing his face. 

And honestly, after all this time—and it feels like it's been a lifetime, carrying this around—I am just so incredibly tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. I'm seventy-six years old. I should be enjoying my retirement, puttering around in my shop, seeing my sister and the grandkids. Instead, I'm constantly doing this mental math, trying to figure out if it's safe to go somewhere or if he's going to be there. It's exhausting. It really is. So, no, there aren't any *other* activities or situations. Just the shop and the family stuff, which, like I said, is a mighty big piece of my life to have to tiptoe around. I just want to stop having to dodge him and that shed all the time.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I suppose I’m here because I just... I can’t keep doing this dance anymore. You see, I’ve got this workshop out back. Built it myself, oh, must have been twenty years ago now, right around the time I stopped working. I used to spend just about every afternoon out there, tinkering with things, staying busy. It was my favorite place to be. But for a good while now, I just... I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop. I won't even walk past the door if I can help it, I'll take the long way around the yard just to avoid looking at it. \n\nAnd it’s not just the shop, it’s my own family, too. If there's a family get-together—a Sunday dinner, a birthday, whatever it might be—I find myself interrogating everybody beforehand to see if my brother-in-law is going to be there. If there is even a slight chance he might show up, I make up some excuse about my back acting up and I just don't go. I stay home. And I’m 76 years old... after all this time, I am just so flat-out tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. It’s exhausting, having to map out every single day, every weekend, just to avoid a room in my own backyard or a dinner with my own relatives. It's just no way to live, always having to plan my schedule around what I can't be near.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you think about going into your workshop or seeing your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s a funny thing, Doc, and not funny haha, you know? It’s just... when I even so much as let my mind drift toward that shed, or toward him, it’s like a switch flips. But the worst part is, I don't even have to be actively thinking about going out there or seeing him for it to hit me. I could be doing just about anything, the most mundane things you can imagine. \n\nTake the mornings, for instance. I’ll be standing there at the bathroom sink, right? Just shaving. I use a brush and a soap puck, the old-fashioned way, always have, ever since I was a teenager. The water is running, nice and warm, and I'm just looking in the mirror, minding my own business, and then... out of nowhere, I'll see his hand. I see his hand gripping that heavy tool again. It’s exactly like a picture that just pops right into my head, clear as day. \n\nOr, you know, I've been trying to do some painting lately. Just small watercolors at the kitchen table to keep my hands busy since I can't go out to the workshop anymore. I'll be sitting there, mixing a color, maybe trying to get a nice sky blue, and suddenly that same picture flashes right in front of my eyes. Just his hand, and that tool. And when it does... oh, boy. My chest just tightens right up. It gets so tight, like someone wrapped a belt around my ribs and pulled it taut, and I usually have to put the razor down, or the paintbrush down, and just grip the edge of the counter or the table until it passes. It’s just... it's always that same image, and it just won't leave me be.\n\nDoctor: What happens to your mood when you see that picture of your brother-in-law's hand in your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, mood... now that’s a tricky word, Doc. I mean, if you’re asking if I get down in the dumps or start crying, or if I get mad enough to kick the dog—not that I have a dog anymore, our old golden retriever Buster passed away about five years ago, bless his heart. Best dog a man could ask for, he used to sit right at my feet while I drank my morning coffee and read the paper. \n\nBut anyway, getting back to my mood... I can’t rightly say it changes my mood, per se. I don't suddenly feel sad or angry or anything like that. It’s not like when the grandkids come over and leave a mess in the living room and I get a little grumpy for the rest of the afternoon, you know? I just... I don't really notice a specific emotion like that, outside of that terrible tightness in my ribs I was just telling you about. \n\nIt’s just that picture flashing, and then that grip in my chest, and I just have to stand there and wait for it to pass. So as far as my actual mood goes—like being happy or sad or irritable or what have you—I honestly couldn't tell you. I just haven't really noticed anything like that happening. It’s more just... it happens, and then once my chest loosens up, I just go right back to trying to finish my shave or my watercolor. I don't think my mood really factors into it at all, to be perfectly honest with you.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of your brother-in-law or something he did that you're trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I suppose it's... it's a bit of both, but mostly it's what happened, or at least what I can remember of what happened. You see, about three years ago, we had this big family barbecue. It was the Fourth of July, hotter than blazes out there, and everybody was over. My sister, her husband—that's the brother-in-law I'm talking about—the kids, the neighbors. We were all out back near the workshop. \n\nNow, he and I, we've never seen eye to eye. We were arguing about something foolish, probably politics or how to properly grill a steak, you know how those things start. But it got heated. Real heated. We ended up inside the workshop, just the two of us, away from the women and the kids. And the shouting... oh, I remember the shouting. It echoed off the tin roof in there. He was red in the face, veins popping out on his neck, screaming at me. \n\nAnd I remember him picking up that heavy wrench. That's the hand I keep seeing, gripping that wrench so tight his knuckles were white. I remember that plain as day. But the funny thing is... well, not funny, but the strange thing is, after that... nothing. It's just a blank spot. I remember the shouting and the tool plain enough, but there's a blank spot right around how it actually ended, like somebody cut a few minutes out of the story. \n\nOne minute he's holding the wrench and yelling, and the next thing I know, I'm sitting on a lawn chair outside, holding a bag of frozen peas to the side of my head, and my sister is crying. I don't remember him swinging it, I don't remember getting hit, I don't remember how I got out of the shop. The doctor at the ER said I had a mild concussion, said it's normal to lose a few minutes of memory with a knock to the head like that. But it's that missing piece, Doc. It's that blank spot. That's what I'm trying to avoid. Because if I go in that shop, or if I see him, I'm terrified that missing piece is going to come flooding back, and I just don't think my old heart could take remembering whatever it is I've forgotten about that day. So I just stay away. It's easier that way, don't you think? Just keeping the whole mess tucked away where I don't have to look at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about what happened with your brother-in-law on that Fourth of July?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as talking or thinking about it goes, I suppose I do try to push it right out of my mind whenever it creeps up, but... well, the real issue, the whole reason I finally made the appointment to come in and sit in this chair and talk to you today, is because of what I'm doing to avoid it all in my day-to-day life. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop. And I mean, I built that shed with my own two hands back in '98, Doc. I poured the concrete foundation myself, framed the walls, even ran the electrical so I could have my lathe and my band saw out there. It used to be my sanctuary, you know? And now... now I'll walk all the way around the long side of the yard, squeezing past where my wife used to keep her prize rose bushes, just so I don't even have to look at the door of the place. \n\nAnd it’s not just the shop, either. It’s the family stuff. Any get-together, any holiday dinner, any birthday party for the nieces and nephews... if there is even a whisper, even a slight chance that my brother-in-law might be there, I just don't go. I make up an excuse. I’ll tell my sister my arthritis is acting up, or I’ve got a plumbing leak to fix, or I just don't feel up to the drive. And I hate lying to her, I really do. She makes this wonderful potato salad, the kind with the red potatoes and the fresh dill, and I haven't had it in three years. \n\nBut after all this time... I'm just tired, Doc. I am so dog-tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. I'm exhausted from mapping out my own backyard and dodging my own family just to make sure I don't see him or that shop. It's like a full-time job, and at my age, I just don't have the energy for it anymore. That's why I came in. I just can't keep living my life tiptoeing around all these landmines.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places or situations, besides your workshop and family gatherings, that you avoid because they remind you of what happened with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that for a second... no, no, not really. Like I was just telling you, it’s mainly just the workshop and the family gatherings. But honestly, Doc, you have to understand that at my age, those two things take up a mighty big chunk of a man's life. When you're retired, you don't have a whole lot of places you have to be every day. Your world gets a little smaller, a little closer to home. So when I say I'm steering clear of my own workshop out back, and any family get-together where my brother-in-law might show his face, well, that's pretty much my whole social calendar right there. \n\nI know I'm repeating myself, but that's exactly why I finally came in to see you today. It's just those two things, but they're everywhere. You wouldn't believe the mental gymnastics it takes to avoid your own backyard. Just the other day, for instance, the hinge on the cabinet under the kitchen sink came loose. The door was hanging all crooked. Now, all my good tools are out in that shop. Every last one of them, organized on a pegboard I put up myself. But instead of just walking thirty feet out the back door to grab a simple Phillips head screwdriver, I actually got my keys, got in my car, drove fifteen minutes down to the hardware store on Main Street, and bought a brand new one. I paid six dollars and forty-nine cents for a tool I already own three of, just so I wouldn't have to go near that shed door. \n\nNow that's just pure foolishness, isn't it? But that's what I'm doing. So, no, there aren't really other places or situations. It's just like I said—I keep steering completely clear of my own workshop and any family get-together where he might be. And after all this time, I am just so tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. It wears a man down, it really does.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the argument with your brother-in-law, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's like a movie playing the whole argument from start to finish, where I'm hearing all the shouting and the echoes in the tin roof again, if that's what you mean. But... well, it's more like a photograph. Out of nowhere, I'll just see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just his hand, gripping it so tight. \n\nAnd it happens at the absolute strangest times, Doc. Times when I'm not even thinking about him or the Fourth of July or any of that mess. Like... well, I could just be standing at the bathroom sink, shaving. I still use one of those old double-edge safety razors, the kind my own father taught me to use, so you really have to pay attention to what you're doing so you don't nick yourself. I'll be looking in the mirror, scraping through the shaving cream, trying to get a close shave around the jawline, and suddenly, like a picture that pops right into my head, there's that hand and that wrench. \n\nOr I'll be working on a painting. I took up watercoloring a while back, mostly sitting at the kitchen table since I don't go out to the shop anymore, just to keep my mind and hands occupied in my retirement. I'll be trying to mix the right shade of green for a tree or something, completely at peace, minding my own business, and then—bam—that image just forces its way in. \n\nAnd I'll tell you, even though it's just a flash in my mind, it still makes my chest tighten up something awful. It gets so tight, like a band is wrapping right around my ribs, that I actually have to put my razor or my paintbrush down and just sit there for a minute. I have to take these long, deep breaths until my heart settles down and the feeling passes. So, I don't know if that counts as reliving the whole argument exactly, but it sure feels like that one terrifying piece of it is happening right there in my kitchen or my bathroom, completely uninvited.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the workshop, reliving the argument with your brother-in-law, with all the sights and sounds coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, Doc, it's not really like I'm standing right back on the sawdust floor hearing all the yelling and the racket. The sounds don't really come back to me like a movie playing out, if that makes sense. It's not the whole scene. I guess I'll try to explain it again, because it's really just that one specific picture. \n\nOut of absolutely nowhere, I'll just see his hand gripping that heavy tool again. Just the hand and the tool, nothing else. And like I mentioned, it sneaks up on me when my mind is completely elsewhere, doing the most mundane things. I could be standing there at the bathroom mirror in the morning, getting my shaving cream all lathered up in my old ceramic mug—the one with the badger hair brush my wife bought me for our anniversary twenty years ago—just trying to get a clean shave around my chin. Or I'll be sitting at the kitchen table, working on one of my watercolors. I might be right in the middle of trying to get the shading right on a landscape, holding a tiny little detail brush, completely relaxed, not a care in the world... \n\nAnd then, boom, that picture just pops right into my head. Just his hand with that heavy tool. And even though there's no sound, no yelling, no echo in the shed... it still does a number on me physically. It still makes my chest tighten up so bad. It gets so tight, like a heavy strap is being pulled around my ribs, that I just have to set my paintbrush down, or put the razor on the edge of the sink, and just sit there for a spell. I have to just breathe through it until my chest stops feeling like it's in a vice grip. So, no, I don't feel like I'm completely back in the workshop with all the noise, but that one image... well, it's enough to knock the wind right out of me, I'll tell you that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're actually back in the moment when your brother-in-law was holding the wrench, like you're reliving it?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... well, I feel like a bit of a broken record here, to be honest with you. Like I've been trying to say, it's not like I'm fully transported back there in body and spirit, reliving the whole afternoon. It's exactly what I was just telling you about. It's just that one picture. \n\nOut of absolutely nowhere, I'll see his hand with that heavy tool again. Just the hand, gripping it. And it pops right into my head like a photograph, usually when I'm doing something completely peaceful and unrelated. Like I said, it happens while I'm shaving with my old safety razor, or when I'm sitting at the kitchen table working on a painting. I could be mixing a nice cerulean blue for a sky, minding my own business, and then—bam. There it is. \n\nAnd I know I already mentioned this, but it's the part that really gets to me, so it bears repeating... it still makes my chest tighten up. It gets so terribly tight I have to put down whatever I'm holding, whether it's the razor or the paintbrush, and just sit and take deep breaths until it passes. So, I don't know if you'd call that actually being back in the moment, but it sure forces its way into my day, plain as day. I really don't know how else to explain it to you, Doc, it's just that one flash, over and over, and it knocks the wind right out of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or the world around you when that picture of your brother-in-law's hand pops into your head?\n\nPatient: Detached? From myself? Well... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I think I know what you mean. You hear about folks who go through something and they talk about feeling like they're floating outside their body, or looking down at themselves from the ceiling like a ghost or something you'd see on one of those old Twilight Zone programs. But for me, it's not like that at all. \n\nIf anything, Doc, I feel a little too stuck in my own body, if you catch my drift. Because like I was just saying—and I know I keep harping on it, but it's the truth—when that picture of his hand pops into my head, I am very much right there in my own skin. I feel that awful tightness in my chest. It's a very real, very physical thing. When I have to put my razor down on the edge of the sink, I can feel the cold porcelain under my fingers. Or if I'm at the kitchen table, I can feel the hard wood of the chair against my back. \n\nI don't lose track of the world around me, either. I'm fully aware of it. I can still hear the refrigerator humming in the background, or the clock ticking in the hallway—we have this old grandfather clock my father left me, it's got a very heavy, loud tick to it, you have to wind it every eight days—and I hear that clear as a bell while I'm sitting there trying to catch my breath. So, no, I wouldn't use the word detached. I know exactly where I am and who I am. I'm just an old fellow trying to get through his morning shave or his painting, waiting for his ribs to stop feeling like they're tied up in knots so he can get on with his day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're actually back in the workshop, reliving the argument, with all the sights and sounds coming back to you?\n\nPatient: Doc, with all due respect, I... I feel like we're going around in circles here. I really do. I know you've got your list of questions there, and you have to ask them, but I feel like I've answered this one two or three times already today. \n\nLike I was just saying—and I'll try to say it as plain as I can—no, I don't feel like I'm back in the workshop. I don't hear the yelling. I don't hear the tools clanking or the echo in that old tin-roof shed. It's not a movie. It's just that one picture of his hand holding the tool. \n\nI'm right here in my house, or wherever I happen to be, just trying to go about my business. I'll be right in the middle of shaving, like I mentioned, with that badger hair brush and the shaving soap—it's a sandalwood soap, comes in a wooden puck, very nice scent, my daughter gets it for me every Christmas—and I'm standing there, fully aware that I'm in my own bathroom. Or I'm at the kitchen table with my watercolors, trying to get a wash just right on some heavy-weight cold-pressed paper. I know exactly where I am. \n\nBut then that image flashes, and my chest gets tight, and I just have to sit there and breathe through it. I hear the grandfather clock ticking, I hear the refrigerator hum. I am not back in the workshop. I'm just an old man sitting in his kitchen with a tight chest, waiting for it to pass so I can pick my paintbrush back up. I really don't know how else to make it any clearer for you, Doc. It's not the whole scene, it's just the one picture.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or daily routines that you avoid because they remind you of the argument with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, there are. And frankly, Doc, that's exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. Because it's getting to be too much, trying to tiptoe around my own life. \n\nIt's the workshop. My own workshop. I used to spend hours out there. I've got a beautiful old Delta table saw, a drill press, a whole wall of hand tools I've collected over fifty years... and I just can't bring myself to go out there anymore. I'll look out the back window at it, and I just... I steer clear. I'll find any excuse not to go in. \"Oh, the grass needs cutting,\" or \"I should really clean the gutters today,\" anything to avoid opening that shed door. My sister—bless her heart, she tries to keep the family close—she hosts a barbecue every Fourth of July, Thanksgiving dinner, Christmas Eve. She goes all out. Bakes these incredible sweet potato pies from our mother's old recipe. But her husband is my brother-in-law, obviously. So if there's even a chance he's going to be there, I just... I make an excuse. I tell them my arthritis is acting up, or I've got a summer cold, or I just don't feel up to the drive. \n\nAnd it breaks my heart, Doc, it really does. I miss seeing my nieces and nephews, I miss my sister's cooking. But the thought of sitting across the table from him, or even just being in the same room and seeing his hands passing the gravy boat... I can't do it. I've been planning my whole life around not running into him, and not going into my own workshop, and after all this time, I'm just plain tired of it. It's exhausting, keeping track of where I can and can't go just to avoid those reminders.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or places you avoid because they remind you of what happened with your brother-in-law?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, honestly... no. Not really any other places. I... I feel like I just laid it all out for you, to be perfectly frank. Like I was just saying, the whole reason I finally picked up the phone and made this appointment to come in and see you in the first place is because of those two things I just went on and on about. \n\nIt's the workshop, and it's any family get-together where my brother-in-law might be. And I know that might not sound like a long list of places to you—maybe you have folks in here who can't even leave their bedrooms or go to the grocery store—but for me, at seventy-six years old, that's a huge chunk of my world. \n\nI mean, you have to understand the toll it takes, constantly being on guard like that. I am just so terribly tired of planning my entire life around not running into those reminders. It's like a full-time job, keeping track of it all. I'll look at the calendar on the fridge—the one the bank gives out for free every January—and I'll see a holiday coming up, or a niece's birthday, and instead of getting excited to see my sister, my stomach just drops. Because I have to start calculating if he's going to be there, and what excuse I'm going to use this time so I don't have to go. Just my own backyard and my own family, which, if you ask me, is more than enough to lose.
